Photograph: Kylie Cooper/Reuters View image in fullscreen Robert F Kennedy Jr, the US health secretary, speaks at the Department of Health and Human Services in Washington DC on 8 June 2026. Photograph: Kylie Cooper/Reuters Vaccine misinformation spreads as RFK Jr suggests two measles deaths were ‘fabricated’ Anti-vaxxers are spreading conspiracy theories after two recent measles-associated deaths in Pennsylvania Anti-vaccine activists are trying to track down the families of two people, including at least one newborn, who died after contracting measles in Pennsylvania, as Governor Josh Shapiro tangles with Robert F Kennedy Jr over his “inaccurate and misleading claims”. Kennedy, the secretary of the US Department of Health and Human Services (HHS), suggested late on Wednesday that the deaths in the state may have been “fabricated” after a Republican coroner – an elected political office in Lancaster county – claimed one of the measles-associated deaths was unrelated to the virus, although the given cause would be consistent with measles. Brian Hooker at Children’s Health Defense, an anti-vaccine organization that until recently was led by Kennedy, said in a recent video that the organization is “trying to get boots on the ground” and “do outreach” in Pennsylvania. Two unvaccinated people die of measles in Pennsylvania Read more Kennedy is “an anti-vaccine activist in the role of the most powerful public health person in this country”, said Paul Offit, an infectious diseases physician at Children’s Hospital of Philadelphia. “It’s like the world’s turned upside down.” It would be helpful to know the deceased patients’ ages and more information about their deaths, he added. “By not having that information, it allows fertile ground for the kind of conspiracy theory that RFK Jr puts out there, where he said in some posts that these weren’t measles deaths.” The US Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) said in a Thursday post on X that it was “working to determine what actually occurred”, including “whether the two reported deaths were caused by measles”. The HHS did not respond to the Guardian’s questions about whether Kennedy recommends the vaccine or whether the newly confirmed CDC director, Erica Schwartz, would make a statement about the outbreak and the need for vaccination. About 424 Pennsylvanians have confirmed measles cases, and 73 have been hospitalized. None of the sickened people are fully vaccinated. The two deaths were in unvaccinated patients in Lancaster county, Pennsylvania, which is home to large Amish and Mennonite communities. While these communities do not have religious objections to vaccines, they eschew many parts of modern life, and thus are relatively disconnected from modern healthcare. Few other details have been released about the patients, fueling a firestorm of misinformation from anti-vaccine activists spreading conspiracy theories about vaccines, which are 97% effective at preventing measles. The anti-vaccine activists are attempting to figure out who died so they can reach the families, as they did in Texas when a six-year-old girl died last year, Hooker said. They filmed a misinformation-filled interview with the Mennonite parents in a rare media appearance after their daughter’s death. Hooker also speculated that the Pennsylvania announcement was a political ploy in advance of the US midterm elections, claiming it was “a strategic move” to “get measles deaths on the tip of everybody’s tongue, to get in the forefront”. The Trump administration has at times attempted to downplay its anti-vaccine moves because they are not popular with voters, according to internal polling. When Shapiro first announced the deaths, he pointed to the inaccurate claims from the highest levels of the US government, saying at a press conference: “There’s real-life consequences to spreading misinformation.” Kennedy responded on X with debunked myths about the measles, mumps and rubella (MMR) vaccine. Shapiro called those claims “inaccurate and misleading” in his own X post. When Kennedy offered to help after the announcement of the two deaths, Shapiro asked him to encourage vaccination and stop spreading misinformation, the governor said in the post. The health secretary responded by repeating vaccine misinformation and saying “it would be better if we went back to ‘natural measles’,” Shapiro wrote. “Secretary Kennedy has stoked confusion amongst parents and others – and the result has been less protection from measles and more cases,” Shapiro added. In 2021, for instance, Kennedy gave a speech praising Amish parents in Lancaster county for not vaccinating their children. “The cure for measles is chicken soup and vitamin A,” he said. There is no cure for measles, only supportive care. A few hours after Shapiro’s response, Kennedy posted to X wondering whether the deaths had been “fabricated” and alleging that Pennsylvania was refusing to share information with the CDC. But Pennsylvania notified the CDC of the deaths early on Tuesday morning, before making the public announcement, Dr Debra Bogen, the state’s secretary of health, said in a statement. “I have thoroughly reviewed the case investigation information and sadly can confirm that there were two recent measles-associated deaths in Lancaster County,” she said. The cases were also thoroughly investigated by the state’s epidemiology team, and both individuals tested positive for measles, she added. One of the deaths was in a newborn who tested positive for measles, the department of health said on Facebook. Stephen Diamantoni, the coroner for Lancaster county, told the Philadelphia Inquirer that a test on the baby’s lung tissue after death indicated a measles infection acquired before birth. He believes the baby died shortly after birth because of a lacerated spleen. Spleen issues are a known complication of measles infection. Having an enlarged spleen from measles “was not uncommon actually in the pre-vaccine era”, Offit said. The scientific literature includes doctors’ observations of enlarged spleens, which can lead to rupture. “If you have an enlarged spleen and you’re infected with measles virus, the measles virus caused your enlarged spleen. If you then have a splenic rupture which kills you, you die from measles,” Offit said. Anti-vaccine activists have been successful in driving down vaccination rates, Offit said. The ensuing outbreak “isn’t a good look” for them, he said. Children’s Health Defense appeared to want to downplay its role and put out “the same kind of bad information” after the children died in Texas, Offit said. “It’s very easy to convince those parents – very easy – that this wasn’t their fault, because otherwise, they would have to take on the responsibility or the notion that they could have prevented their child’s death.” Hooker complained on Wednesday that the scant details released about the patients made it difficult to reach them. He also noted that his organization was unable to contact the family of a measles-positive adult who died in New Mexico last year, because the state did not release more public information. “The more information that they release regarding these particular deaths, the more that we would be able to dig in and contact family members, get access to medical records and see exactly what happened,” said Hooker. Children’s Health Defense has claimed that one of the child deaths in Texas last year was due to medical error, not measles, despite findings from officials on measles. Measles deaths have become rare in the US because of vaccination, but between one and three people usually die with every 1,000 infections. The US is now experiencing its worst outbreak of measles in three decades. It is unusual for measles to spread in summer months like this – and now children are heading back to school, potentially amplifying transmission, Offit said. “You’re just going to see, I think, an explosion of measles.” Infants under the age of six months are too young to be vaccinated against measles, which is a large reason why it is important to vaccinate pregnant parents and others in the community, Offit said. More than 35,000 Pennsylvanians got vaccinated against measles last month – an increase of 10,000 compared to typical months. 美国麻疹危机在2026年首现死亡病例,局势恶化 - Axios

U.S. measles crisis deepens with first 2026 deaths - Axios
新闻简报据宾夕法尼亚州卫生部门报告,美国麻疹危机在2026年首次出现死亡病例,局势进一步恶化。兰开斯特县两名未接种疫苗的个体死于麻疹,这是该州30多年来首次出现此类死亡病例。麻疹已导致至少70人住院,截至2026年8月,美国报告麻疹病例2,777例,已超过去年全年总数。美国疾病控制与预防中心(CDC)强调,大多数病例涉及未接种疫苗的个体,并强调对无免疫力人群构成的风险。美国麻疹消除状态将于2026年11月接受审查,卫生专家警告称,将MMR疫苗拆分为三剂可能增加麻疹风险。此次麻疹疫情凸显疫苗接种(疫苗接种)在预防麻疹传播中的重要性,麻疹是一种高度传染性疾病,在美国已基本被消除超过25年。

B routine priorityPublic-health news

U.S. measles crisis deepens with first 2026 deaths - Axios

News Brief
The U.S. measles crisis has deepened with the first reported deaths in 2026, according to Pennsylvania's health department. Two unvaccinated individuals in Lancaster County died from measles, marking the first such deaths in the state in over three decades. The outbreak has led to at least 70 hospitalizations, with the U.S. reporting 2,777 measles cases by August 2026, surpassing last year's total. The CDC highlights that most cases involve unvaccinated individuals, emphasizing the risk posed to those without immunity. The measles elimination status in the U.S. is scheduled for review in November 2026, and health experts have warned that splitting the MMR vaccine into three shots could increase the risk of outbreaks. The outbreak underscores the importance of vaccination in preventing the spread of measles, a highly contagious disease that was largely eliminated in the U.S. over 25 years ago.

Add Axios on Google A colorized transmission electron micrograph showing measles virus and SV40 particles. Photo: Erskine Palmer/CDC/Smith Collection/Gado/Getty Images Two people in Pennsylvania have died from measles, the state's health department said Tuesday — they are the state's first deaths from the illness in more than three decades. Why it matters: The U.S. is currently experiencing its highest count of measles cases in 35 years. The two deaths are the first reported in the U.S. in 2026. Measles is a highly contagious disease that spreads through coughing or breathing. For years, it was a common childhood illness before a vaccine was developed to protect against it. Anyone who is unprotected from measles is at risk of getting it. Driving the news: The two people who died in Pennsylvania were unvaccinated, the state's health department said. They lived in Lancaster County, the epicenter of the state's outbreak this year, which has seen at least 390 cases, the health department said. Lancaster County has historically had lower vaccination rates compared to the rest of the state. The state's outbreak has caused at least 70 hospitalizations. What they're saying: "Because measles was largely eliminated in the Commonwealth for more than three decades, people are not familiar with this disease and don't fully understand the potential severity of the illness," Pennsylvania Secretary of Health Dr. Debra Bogen wrote in a statement. Measles cases hit 35-year high in U.S. By the numbers: The Centers for Disease Control and Prevention says there have been at least 2,777 measles cases reported in the U.S. this year. The U.S. surpassed last year's total for measles cases in July. Three people died from measles in 2025, a year that saw widespread outbreaks throughout the nation. Measles outbreak and the MMR vaccine, explained Zoom out: The measles, mumps and rubella (MMR) vaccine protects against measles by supercharging the immune system, providing 97% protection for life. Most of the people infected with measles are unvaccinated, the CDC says. "When measles gets into communities of unvaccinated people in the United States, outbreaks can occur," the CDC says. State of play: President Trump announced an executive order in August to split the childhood MMR vaccine into three shots, delivered at separate medical visits. Health experts warned that breaking up the vaccine shots will make children less safe, decrease interest in vaccination and leave communities more exposed to measles outbreaks. U.S. elimination status under threat The big picture: The rise in measles cases last year challenged the U.S. elimination status for measles for the first time. Measles was declared eliminated in the U.S. 26 years ago, largely thanks to the country's vaccination program. Many health experts expect the U.S. to lose its status in 2026, given the swarm of cases this year. What's next: The U.S. measles elimination status is scheduled for review in November. 俄亥俄州新增麻疹病例减少,宾夕法尼亚州报告两例死亡——《哥伦布信使报》

New measles cases in Ohio slow as Pennsylvania reports two deaths - The Columbus Dispatch
新闻简报2026年8月,俄亥俄州报告了该年度第三次麻疹 暴发,全州12个县共报告107例病例。暴发传播速度已放缓,过去一周仅报告8例新增病例。同期,宾夕法尼亚州报告了两例与麻疹相关的死亡病例,这是该州35年来首次出现此类死亡病例,也是今年美国首次报告此类死亡。这两例死亡均与未接种疫苗的个体相关,凸显了在疫苗接种覆盖率低的社区中麻疹的风险。全国范围内,麻疹病例已达35年来最高水平,2026年累计报告2,295例感染,超过2025年总数。卫生官员将疫情激增归因于疫苗接种覆盖率下降及反疫苗运动的影响。他们呼吁接种MMR疫苗以预防暴发,保护脆弱人群,包括5岁以下儿童、20岁以上成人、孕妇及免疫功能受损者。俄亥俄州卫生部门指出,2023年该州儿童及青少年疫苗接种覆盖率为91.5%,低于群体免疫阈值95%。美国疾病控制与预防中心(CDC)及约翰斯·霍普金斯大学强调了麻疹的死灰复燃,数据显示自2025年以来病例显著增加。专家警告称,将MMR疫苗拆分为三次注射会增加注射疼痛并延长儿童未受保护的时间,可能进一步降低整体疫苗接种覆盖率。
查看新闻五要素
时间
暴发在俄亥俄州传播速度放缓,过去一周仅报告8例新增病例;而宾夕法尼亚州在2026年8月报告了两例麻疹相关死亡病例。
地点与对象
暴发发生于俄亥俄州,涉及阿什塔比拉、库亚霍加、富兰克林、吉奥加、哈里森、霍姆斯、诺克斯、米格斯、渥太华、特朗布尔、图斯卡罗瓦斯及韦恩共12个县。受影响人群年龄范围为15个月至20岁。
事件
2026年,俄亥俄州报告了该年度第三次麻疹 暴发,全州12个县共报告107例病例。宾夕法尼亚州报告了两例麻疹相关死亡病例,这是该州35年来首次出现此类死亡,也是今年美国首次报告此类死亡。
规模、影响与风险
全国范围内,麻疹病例达35年来最高水平,2026年累计报告2,295例,超过2025年总数。俄亥俄州疫情虽有放缓但仍在持续,共报告107例病例。宾夕法尼亚州的两例死亡均与未接种疫苗的个体相关,凸显了疫苗接种覆盖率低的社区面临的麻疹风险。
应对、状态与不确定性
俄亥俄州及宾夕法尼亚州卫生官员正在监测暴发,将疫情上升归因于疫苗接种覆盖率下降。他们呼吁接种MMR疫苗进行预防。CDC及约翰斯·霍普金斯大学提供了全国麻疹死灰复燃的数据,专家就反疫苗运动的影响及维持群体免疫的重要性发表了评论。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

New measles cases in Ohio slow as Pennsylvania reports two deaths - The Columbus Dispatch

News Brief
In August 2026, Ohio experienced its third measles outbreak of the year, with 107 cases reported across 12 counties. The outbreak has slowed, with only eight new cases reported in the past week. Meanwhile, Pennsylvania reported two measles-related deaths, marking the first in the state in 35 years and the first in the U.S. this year. These deaths were linked to unvaccinated individuals, emphasizing the risk of measles in communities with low vaccination rates. Nationwide, measles cases have reached a 35-year high, with 2,295 infections reported in 2026, surpassing the total for 2025. Health officials attribute the surge to declining vaccination rates and the influence of anti-vaccine movements. They are urging the MMR vaccine to prevent outbreaks and protect vulnerable populations, including children under 5, adults over 20, pregnant women, and those with compromised immune systems. The Ohio Department of Health noted that vaccination coverage for children and adolescents was 91.5% in 2023, below the herd immunity threshold of 95%. The CDC and Johns Hopkins University have highlighted the resurgence of measles, with data showing a significant increase in cases since 2025. Experts warn that separating the MMR vaccine into three shots increases injection pain and leaves children unprotected longer, potentially lowering overall vaccination rates.
Original source text (English)
News Sports Business Opinion Real Estate Advertise Obituaries eNewspaper Legals HEALTHCARE New measles cases in Ohio slow as Pennsylvania reports two deaths Chad Murphy James Powel Mary Walrath-Holdridge USA TODAY NETWORK Aug. 28, 2026, 4:49 a.m. ET Ohio's third measles outbreak of 2026 has grown to 107 cases across 12 counties. Nationwide, measles cases have reached a 35-year high, with two recent deaths reported in Pennsylvania. Health officials attribute the rise in cases to declining vaccination rates and encourage the MMR vaccine for protection. Ohio's third measles outbreak of 2026 continues to grow, but has slowed down with only eight new cases reported over the past week. The Ohio Department of Health is reporting 107 cases in 12 counties, according to its summary of infectious diseases dashboard, updated Aug. 20. That's up from 99 cases in 12 counties on Aug. 20; 69 cases in 11 counties on Aug. 13; and 45 cases in 10 counties on Aug. 6. “It’s unfortunate that these outbreaks have become more common in Ohio over the past few years, because this disease is preventable,” Dr. Bruce Vanderhoff, ODH director, said a July 30 statement. “This serves to emphasize the importance of being fully vaccinated. I strongly encourage you to protect yourselves and your children by getting vaccinated.” Nationwide, two people in Pennsylvania died from measles, the first of the year, USA TODAY reports. And measles cases nationwide hit a 35-year high for a disease once declared eradicated in the United States. Data released July 22 by the Johns Hopkins University shows 2,295 measles cases have been reported nationwide so far this year, surpassing the 2,286 infections recorded during all of 2025 and marking the highest annual total since 1991. Measles growing in Ohio; counties reporting cases Ohio counties reporting measles cases since Jan. 1, 2026, include: Ashtabula: 24 Cuyahoga: 3 Franklin: 7 Geauga: 28 Harrison: 10 Holmes: 5 Knox: 4 Meigs: 4 Ottawa: 1 Trumbull: 3 Tuscarawas: 12 Wayne: 6 The case count reflects two previous outbreaks in Cuyahoga County in January and Franklin County in February. The ages of those with confirmed cases range from 15 months to 20 years old, according to various health departments. Measles cases have been growing in Ohio over the past several years, with 45 cases in 2025, seven in 2024 and one case in 2023. Pennsylvania measles deaths At least two people have died from measles in Pennsylvania, the state's health department announced. Both were unvaccinated. The measles deaths were the first in Pennsylvania in 35 years, the state Department of Health said in a news release Aug. 25, and the first such deaths in the United States this year, according to Centers for Disease Control and Prevention data. Where are measles cases spreading? While Harrison County Public Health reported one case of measles as of Aug. 7, the state dashboard shows two additional cases there. The Wayne County Health Department reports some of its cases are likely associated with a previously identified measles exposure event at Smokey Lane Stables, 686 Smokey Lane Road NW in Sugarcreek, on July 17. One of the Tuscarawas cases is from the same event, that county health department reports. Geauga Public Health reports that the individuals with measles visited several locations while able to spread the virus, including Bloomfield Horse Sale, 2211 Kinsman Road NW, on July 18. The Tuscarawas health department reports that another of its cases was exposed at the Bloomfield Livestock Auction, at the same address as Bloomfield Horse Sale, on on the same day. In Trumbull County, health officials report that infected individuals were at the Walmart in Liberty on July 29 and Akron Children's Mahoning Valley Emergency Department in Boardman on Aug. 2, during the time they were able to spread the virus. Measles cases hit 35-year high in United States The United States declared measles eliminated in 2000 after recording just 86 cases that year, according to USA TODAY, citing the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention. But a series of outbreaks that began in early 2025, centered initially in Gaines County, Texas, has helped drive the virus' resurgence. Health experts say the recent surge has been fueled by declining vaccination rates that have left more communities vulnerable to outbreaks. Why are measles cases on the rise? Ohio vaccination rates Vaccination for measles has declined in recent years, resulting in a growing number of states no longer reporting rates consistent with herd immunity. Herd immunity occurs when more than 95% of people in a community are vaccinated. Most people are then protected, even if they cannot get the vaccine themselves, because those around them are immune and cannot spread the disease. Vaccination coverage among U.S. kindergartners decreased from 95.2% during the 2019–20 school year to 92.5% in the 2024–25 school year, well under the threshold necessary for effective herd immunity, according to the CDC. Declining protections have also been attributed to the growing anti-vaccine movement, which was bolstered by the confirmation of vaccine skeptic Robert F. Kennedy Jr. as health secretary. In addition to making anti-vaccine comments, Kennedy also downplayed pediatric deaths related to measles and touted false cures and preventives, such as the use of vitamin A. During the height of the Texas outbreak, Kennedy encouraged inoculation against measles, pulling back from his previous anti-vaccine messaging. Kennedy is now encouraging the measles vaccine. In Ohio, MMR vaccine — which protects against measles, mumps and rubella — coverage rate for children and adolescents was 91.5% for 2023, the most recent year available from ODH. Previous years ranged from an 80% to 90% immunization rate: 2019: 81.8% 2020: 88.0% 2021: 91.7% 2022: 89.0% Trump signs executive order to separate MMR vaccine President Donald Trump signed an executive order to split the childhood measles, mumps and rubella vaccine into three shots and delivering them at separate medical visits as well as "calling for more vaccine flexibility and research" and fewer childhood vaccinations, USA TODAY reports. "You have the MMR, we want it in three separate vaccinations, given at separate times. Together there could be a possibility they're quite lethal and separately, it looks like they are not at all lethal, but just very effective," said Trump from the Oval Office as he signed the order. But the MMR vaccine is not lethal, and medical research shows no health benefit or reduced risk from splitting it. In contrast, separating the shots increases injection pain, leaves children unprotected longer, and lower overall vaccination rates, according to experts. Can you get measles if vaccinated? Two doses of MMR are 97% effective against measles, ODH reports. If you are up to date on the measles vaccine, the risk of getting sick is very low. Are measles deadly? Measles can be deadly. ODH states that complications from measles are more common among children younger than 5 years of age, adults older than 20 years of age, pregnant women, and people with compromised immune systems. As many as one out of every 20 children with measles gets pneumonia, the most common cause of death from measles in young children. What does measles look like? Signs, symptoms Symptoms usually appear seven to 14 days after exposure, but it can take up to 21 days. Symptoms include a rash, high fever, runny nose, cough, loss of appetite and red, watery eyes. The rash usually lasts five to six days and begins at the hairline, moves to the face and upper neck, and proceeds down the body. Diarrhea and ear infections are common complications of measles. More severe complications such as pneumonia and encephalitis, a swelling of the brain, may also occur. The measles virus can live for up to two hours in air where an infected person coughed or sneezed. If other people breathe the contaminated air or touch an infected surface and then touch their eyes, noses, or mouths, they can become infected. People infected with measles can spread it to others from four days before, through four days after, a rash appears. Are measles itchy? Not usually, according to the Cleveland Clinic. How to protect yourself against measles Both ODH and the CDC say the best way to protect yourself against measles is to be vaccinated. USA TODAY reporters James Powel and Mary Walrath-Holdridge contributed to this report. The outbreak in Ohio slowed down with only eight new cases reported over the past week, while Pennsylvania reported two deaths from measles in August 2026.
Location and population
The outbreak occurred in Ohio, specifically in 12 counties, with cases reported in Ashtabula, Cuyahoga, Franklin, Geauga, Harrison, Holmes, Knox, Meigs, Ottawa, Trumbull, Tuscarawas, and Wayne. The affected population included individuals ranging in age from 15 months to 20 years old.
Event
Ohio experienced its third measles outbreak of 2026, with 107 cases reported across 12 counties. Pennsylvania reported two deaths from measles, the first in the state in 35 years and the first in the U.S.
Scale, impact and risk
Nationwide, measles cases reached a 35-year high with 2,295 cases reported in 2026, surpassing the total for 2025. In Ohio, the outbreak has slowed but remains active, with 107 cases. The deaths in Pennsylvania were attributed to unvaccinated individuals, highlighting the risk of measles in communities with low vaccination rates.

Health officials in Ohio and Pennsylvania are monitoring the outbreak, attributing the rise in cases to declining vaccination rates. They are encouraging the MMR vaccine for prevention. The CDC and Johns Hopkins University have provided data on the national resurgence of measles, and experts have commented on the impact of anti-vaccine movements and the importance of maintaining herd immunity.

宾夕法尼亚州两名未接种疫苗者死于麻疹,美国病例数达数十年来最高水平

Two Unvaccinated People Die of Measles in Pennsylvania as U.S. Cases Reach Highest Level in Decades
新闻简报Two unvaccinated residents of Lancaster County, Pennsylvania, have died from measles, marking the first U.S. measles-associated fatalities in 2026 and Pennsylvania’s first such deaths in 35 years, state health officials reported on August 25. The fatalities coincide with a nationwide surge in measles cases, with 2,777 confirmed infections through August 20—already surpassing the 2,289 recorded for all of 2025—94% of which were outbreak-linked. Pennsylvania alone has tallied 393 cases across 28 counties. CDC data show kindergarten MMR vaccination coverage fell to 92.4% in 2025–2026 from 95.2% in 2019–2020, leaving roughly 280,000 children without full protection. Measles can lead to severe complications, including pneumonia and brain inflammation, with hospitalization in about one in five patients and death in 1–3 per 1,000 cases.

地点与对象
宾夕法尼亚州兰开斯特县,美国;美国

宾夕法尼亚州兰开斯特县两名未接种疫苗的居民死于麻疹,这是2026年美国首次报告与麻疹相关的死亡病例,也是宾州35年来首次出现此类死亡病例。宾夕法尼亚州卫生部于2026年8月25日通报此事。
规模、影响与风险
Two deaths in Lancaster County, Pennsylvania; U.S. measles cases reached 2,777 through August 20, 2026, exceeding the 2,289 cases reported for all of 2025; 94% of 2026 cases were outbreak-associated; Pennsylvania reported 393 cases in 28 counties as of August 25, 2026; CDC estimates 280,000 kindergarteners nationally were not fully protected against measles in 2025–2026; measles can cause severe disease including pneumonia and brain inflammation; approximately 20% of measles patients are hospitalized and death occurs in about 1–3 per 1,000 cases.
应对、状态与不确定性
宾夕法尼亚州卫生部与CDC通报了死亡病例与病例数量;美国幼儿园儿童麻疹疫苗(MMR)接种覆盖率从2019–2020学年的95.2%降至2025–2026学年的92.4%;CDC指出社区需至少达到95%的两剂次疫苗接种覆盖率才能预防暴发;2024–2025学年各州接种覆盖率从爱达荷州的78.5%至康涅狄格州的98.2%不等;为保护隐私,未披露更多受害者信息;调查仍在进行中;除标准报告外,无其他数据来源限制。

Two Unvaccinated People Die of Measles in Pennsylvania as U.S. Cases Reach Highest Level in Decades

News Brief
Two unvaccinated residents of Lancaster County, Pennsylvania, have died from measles, marking the first U.S. measles-associated fatalities in 2026 and Pennsylvania’s first such deaths in 35 years, state health officials reported on August 25. The fatalities coincide with a nationwide surge in measles cases, with 2,777 confirmed infections through August 20—already surpassing the 2,289 recorded for all of 2025—94% of which were outbreak-linked. Pennsylvania alone has tallied 393 cases across 28 counties. CDC data show kindergarten MMR vaccination coverage fell to 92.4% in 2025–2026 from 95.2% in 2019–2020, leaving roughly 280,000 children without full protection. Measles can lead to severe complications, including pneumonia and brain inflammation, with hospitalization in about one in five patients and death in 1–3 per 1,000 cases.

Share on FacebookShare on Twitter Two Unvaccinated People Die of Measles in Pennsylvania as U.S. Outbreak Surpasses 2025 Total Two unvaccinated residents of Lancaster County, Pennsylvania, have died from measles, the Pennsylvania Department of Health said Tuesday, marking the first measles-associated deaths reported in the United States in 2026. The deaths are also the first measles-related deaths in Pennsylvania in 35 years, according to the state health department. Officials said both victims were unvaccinated and lived in Lancaster County. They did not release additional information about the victims to protect their privacy. The deaths come as the United States experiences its largest measles outbreak in decades. As of Aug. 20, the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention had recorded 2,777 confirmed measles cases in the United States in 2026, compared with 2,289 for all of 2025. The CDC said 94% of this year’s confirmed cases were associated with outbreaks. Pennsylvania had reported 393 measles cases in 28 counties as of Aug. 25, according to the state Department of Health. Pennsylvania records first measles deaths in 35 years The two deaths represent a significant reversal for Pennsylvania, where measles deaths had not been reported for more than three decades. Both victims were unvaccinated Lancaster County residents, the state health department said. Pennsylvania officials have emphasized that measles can cause severe disease, including pneumonia and inflammation of the brain. The Pennsylvania Department of Health estimates that nearly 20% of people who contract measles are hospitalized. It also says measles-related death occurs in approximately one to three patients per 1,000 cases. Measles is exceptionally contagious. The virus spreads through the air when an infected person breathes, coughs or sneezes and can remain infectious in the air or on surfaces for up to two hours after the infected person leaves, according to Pennsylvania health officials. Two doses of the measles, mumps and rubella vaccine are approximately 97% effective against measles, according to the CDC. One dose is approximately 93% effective. U.S. measles cases have risen sharply The 2,777 confirmed cases reported through Aug. 20 already exceed the 2,289 cases recorded during the entire 2025 calendar year. The CDC reported 285 cases in 2024, 59 in 2023 and 121 in 2022. The contrast with the current outbreak is stark. Measles was declared eliminated from the United States in 2000. In public-health terms, elimination means that continuous transmission of measles had been interrupted in the country. It did not mean that the virus had disappeared globally or that Americans could no longer become infected. The United States continues to record imported cases, particularly among travelers returning from countries where measles is circulating. When the virus reaches communities with insufficient vaccination coverage, sustained outbreaks can develop. Five U.S. measles deaths in 2025 and 2026 The two Pennsylvania deaths follow three confirmed measles deaths in the United States in 2025. The CDC reported that two school-aged children in Texas and one adult in New Mexico died from measles in 2025. All three were unvaccinated. The CDC’s detailed review of the 2025 outbreak found that the two Texas children had no known underlying medical conditions. The New Mexico death involved an unvaccinated adult. CDC officials have noted that deaths from measles had been exceptionally rare in the United States after elimination. From 2001 through 2024, only three measles deaths were reported nationally — two in 2003 and one in 2015. That means the United States has now recorded five measles-associated deaths in 2025 and 2026, compared with three during the previous 24 years combined. U.S. vaccination coverage has fallen below the measles protection threshold The decline in measles vaccination coverage is one of the central public-health concerns behind the current outbreak. CDC data show that the percentage of U.S. kindergarten children who had received the recommended two doses of MMR fell from 95.2% in the 2019–2020 school year to 92.4% in 2025–2026. The CDC estimates that approximately 280,000 kindergarten children were not fully protected against measles during the 2025–2026 school year. The CDC says communities generally need vaccination coverage of at least 95% with two doses to maintain the level of population immunity needed to prevent measles outbreaks. The national average, however, conceals substantial differences among states and individual communities. The gap between U.S. states is wide The latest fully published CDC school-year data previously showed MMR coverage among kindergarteners ranging from 78.5% in Idaho to 98.2% in Connecticut during 2024–2025. Only 10 states and jurisdictions reached coverage above the 95% benchmark that year. State Two-dose MMR coverage among kindergartners, 2024–25 Connecticut 98.2% Idaho 78.5% U.S. overall 92.5% The difference between the two states is nearly 20 percentage points. Connecticut reported the nation’s highest kindergarten MMR coverage at 98.2% in 2024–25, according to state data based on CDC reporting. Idaho’s coverage was only 78.5%. State health data show that Idaho also had a substantially higher overall kindergarten vaccine-exemption rate — 15.4%, compared with 3.6% nationally, in 2024–25. RelatedPosts Pancreatic Cancer Vaccine Shows New Promise as Researchers Seek a Way to Outsmart One of the Deadliest Cancers FDA Approves First Oral Drug Indicated to Treat Dermatomyositis in Adults Dr. Irfan Atcha Explains Why the Best Decisions in Dentistry Often Happen Before Treatment FDA Authorizes First Wearable Device That Continuously Monitors Both Ketone Levels and Blood Sugar These figures demonstrate why national averages can be misleading. A state can have vaccination coverage near the national average while individual counties, schools or communities have substantially lower immunity. Pennsylvania is below the national protection threshold Pennsylvania’s kindergarten MMR coverage is also below the 95% threshold. See also Pancreatic Cancer Vaccine Shows New Promise as Researchers Seek a Way to Outsmart One of the Deadliest Cancers State data for the 2025–2026 school year put Pennsylvania’s two-dose MMR coverage at about 93%, according to reporting based on the latest CDC school-vaccination data. Lancaster County, where the current outbreak is concentrated, has an even lower rate, reported at 87.6%. The difference between Pennsylvania’s statewide rate and Lancaster County’s rate illustrates the importance of local vaccination coverage. The CDC specifically warns that pockets of unvaccinated people can exist even in states with relatively high overall vaccination rates. When measles is introduced into such a community, transmission can accelerate rapidly. The vaccination gap between U.S. states is substantial The national vaccination rate obscures major differences between states. During the 2024–2025 school year, MMR coverage among U.S. kindergartners was 92.5%, below the 95% level generally considered necessary to maintain community protection against measles. But state-level coverage ranged from 78.5% in Idaho to 98.2% in Connecticut. Only 10 states or jurisdictions reached at least 95%. State Two-dose MMR coverage, 2024–25 Connecticut 98.2% California 96.1% Alabama 95.2% Pennsylvania 93.2% Illinois 91.8% Indiana 90.8% Iowa 88.8% Florida 88.8% Colorado 88.0% Georgia 86.8% Alaska 81.2% Idaho 78.5% U.S. average 92.5% Vaccine exemptions have also increased CDC data show that 3.6% of U.S. kindergarten children had an exemption from one or more required vaccines during the 2024–2025 school year, up from 3.3% the previous year. The CDC estimated that approximately 138,000 kindergarten children had an exemption from at least one vaccine. Exemption rates increased in 36 states and the District of Columbia, and 17 states reported exemption rates above 5%. The existence of an exemption does not necessarily mean that a child is unvaccinated against measles, because exemptions can apply to individual vaccines and may be medical or, depending on state law, nonmedical. Europe is facing an even larger measles burden The United States is not the only region experiencing a resurgence of measles. WHO and UNICEF reported 33,998 measles cases across Europe and Central Asia in 2025, based on preliminary data from 53 countries. That represented a nearly 75% decline from 127,412 cases in 2024, but remained far above the levels seen during most years since 2000. The 2024 outbreak was particularly severe. The WHO European Region recorded 127,350 measles cases in 2024, the highest annual number since 1997. More than half of those patients required hospitalization, and 38 deaths were reported based on preliminary data available in March 2025. The WHO European Region covers 53 countries in Europe and Central Asia, meaning its figures cannot be directly compared with U.S. totals as though they represented the same population. WHO and UNICEF said vaccination gaps remain a major concern. Between 2019 and 2024, coverage with the second dose of measles-containing vaccine in the European Region declined from 92% to 91%. WHO says every community should reach approximately 95% two-dose measles vaccination coverage to prevent outbreaks. Europe has countries with substantial vaccination gaps The European picture also varies significantly from country to country. The WHO does not treat Europe as a single uniformly vaccinated population. Its surveillance system tracks vaccination and measles transmission at the country level, while outbreaks often occur in communities where immunity is considerably below the regional average. WHO reported that, based on 2024 country data, the number of countries in the European Region with continuing or re-established endemic measles transmission increased to 19, from 12 the previous year. The organization has warned that declining coverage, delayed vaccination and immunity gaps are allowing measles to return to communities that had previously controlled or eliminated transmission. Measles deaths are overwhelmingly concentrated among people without adequate protection The available U.S. surveillance data show a strong relationship between measles illness and lack of vaccination. In the CDC’s analysis of the first 800 U.S. cases reported in 2025, 96% of patients were either unvaccinated or had an unknown vaccination status. Excluding Texas, where vaccination-status reporting had limitations, 77% of patients were confirmed unvaccinated, 6% had received two doses, 3% had received one dose and 14% had unknown vaccination status. Among hospitalized patients in that analysis, 66% were unvaccinated, 33% had unknown vaccination status and 1% had received one MMR dose. The 2025 deaths were also reported among unvaccinated people, as are the two Pennsylvania deaths announced this week. That does not mean every measles infection in an unvaccinated person results from a deliberate refusal to vaccinate. Infants may be too young for routine vaccination, some people have medical contraindications, and vaccination records can sometimes be unavailable. For that reason, public-health agencies generally report vaccination status, rather than classifying individual deaths as resulting from “negligence.” The historical toll of measles shows what vaccination changed Before widespread measles vaccination, the disease was common in the United States. The CDC estimates that before the measles vaccine was introduced, approximately 48,000 people were hospitalized and 400 to 500 died from measles each year in the United States. The disease did not become less dangerous because it was eliminated. Its impact fell because vaccination dramatically reduced the number of susceptible people. The CDC estimates that two MMR doses provide about 97% protection against measles. Worldwide, the difference is even more dramatic. WHO estimates that measles vaccination has prevented tens of millions of deaths since 2000, while measles still kills children when vaccination coverage falls and outbreaks become established. WHO’s most recent global immunization data show that 84% of children received a first measles-containing vaccine dose by age 2 in 2025, while 77% received a second dose. B routine priorityPublic-health news

Pennsylvania's MMR vaccination rate fell below 95%. Now two residents have died from measles - The Economic Times

News Brief
In Pennsylvania, two residents have died from measles, marking the first measles-related deaths in the state in 35 years. This outbreak, which has seen 393 cases reported across 28 counties in 2026, is part of a broader resurgence of measles in the United States, with 2,777 confirmed cases as of August 20, 2026. The deaths occurred among unvaccinated individuals in Lancaster County, highlighting the risks associated with low vaccination rates. The Pennsylvania Department of Health estimates that one to three people die for every 1,000 measles cases, and the virus can lead to severe complications such as pneumonia and brain swelling. The outbreak underscores the importance of maintaining a 95% vaccination rate to prevent sustained transmission, as the state's vaccination rates have fallen below this threshold in several schools. Health officials have not disclosed details about the victims due to privacy concerns, but the incident serves as a reminder of the dangers of measles when immunity levels decline.

Now two residents have died from measles SECTIONS Pennsylvania's MMR vaccination rate fell below 95%. Now two residents have died from measles ET OnlineLast Updated: 25 August, 2026 09:57 AM GMT Rate Story Follow us Share Font Size AbcSmall AbcMedium AbcLarge Save Print Comment Synopsis Pennsylvania is facing its first measles fatalities in thirty-five years due to a rise in cases statewide, primarily affecting unvaccinated individuals in Lancaster County. This alarming outbreak underscores the urgent need for improved vaccination rates across the Commonwealth, as national measles cases in 2026 reach levels not seen since the early 1990s. AP Measles is a highly contagious viral infection that spreads through the air when an infected person coughs, sneezes or breathes. Two Pennsylvania residents have died from measles, marking the state's first measles-related deaths in 35 years as cases continue to rise across the Commonwealth. Health officials said Tuesday that both people were unvaccinated residents of Lancaster County. The Pennsylvania Department of Health did not release additional information about the victims, citing privacy concerns. The deaths come as Pennsylvania has reported 393 measles cases across 28 counties in 2026, underscoring how quickly the highly contagious virus can spread when it reaches communities with lower vaccination coverage. by Taboolaby Taboola Sponsored LinksSponsored Links Promoted LinksPromoted Links You May Like ₹29K rent in Noida: Woman shows her semi-furnished flat with swimming pool, gym and other amenitiesThe Economic Times Undo "Because measles was largely eliminated in the Commonwealth for more than three decades, people are not familiar with this disease and don't fully understand the potential severity of the illness," Pennsylvania Health Secretary Debra Bogen said. The outbreak is part of a broader resurgence of measles across the United States. The Centers for Disease Control and Prevention had recorded 2,777 confirmed U.S. cases as of August 20, making 2026 the worst year for measles infections since the major resurgence between 1989 and 1991. Live Events That earlier period saw more than 55,000 infections and 123 deaths. Why the 95% vaccination rate matters The Pennsylvania deaths come as newly released school immunization data show that dozens of schools across northeastern and central Pennsylvania have kindergarten MMR vaccination rates below 95%, with several reporting rates of 80% or lower. The 95% figure is an important benchmark for measles because the virus is exceptionally contagious. Public-health experts estimate that roughly 95% of a population needs immunity to prevent sustained transmission. When vaccination coverage falls below that level, pockets of vulnerable people can allow the virus to spread more easily after an infected person enters a community. That makes school vaccination data particularly important because children are among those most affected by measles outbreaks. What makes measles so dangerous? Measles is a highly contagious viral infection that spreads through the air when an infected person coughs, sneezes or breathes. The virus can cause complications including pneumonia and brain swelling. According to Pennsylvania health officials, nearly 20% of people who contract measles are hospitalized. Deaths remain uncommon but can occur, with the Pennsylvania Department of Health estimating that roughly one to three people die for every 1,000 measles cases. The MMR vaccine is typically administered in two doses during childhood, around ages 1 and 5. Adults and teenagers who are not fully vaccinated are also advised to ensure they are up to date. Is the MMR vaccine safe? The MMR vaccine has been used in its current form since 1971 and has been administered to millions of people. Serious reactions are rare, while vaccination has played a major role in reducing measles, mumps and rubella in the United States. Before the measles vaccine was introduced in 1963, an estimated 3 million to 4 million Americans contracted measles every year, resulting in about 500 deaths and 48,000 hospitalizations annually. U.S. measles cases have reached a decades-high level The Pennsylvania deaths come after U.S. measles cases surpassed the previous year's total. CDC data show that 93% of reported cases in 2026 occurred among unvaccinated people, while nearly 69% involved children and teenagers ages 19 and younger. The country had already experienced a 30-year high in 2025, when 2,289 confirmed cases were reported across 43 states and Washington, D.C. Nearly 90% of those infections were connected to 48 known outbreaks. Three people, including two children, died during the 2025 outbreaks, marking the first pediatric measles deaths in the United States in a decade. For Pennsylvania, however, the latest deaths carry another distinction: the state had gone 35 years without a reported measles death. The return of fatal cases is a reminder of how quickly a disease once considered largely eliminated can regain a foothold when immunity levels decline. 6 Months, 27 Products: These 5 Whitening Picks Stood OutWe Tested 27 Teeth Whitening Products—These 5 Made the CutBloomWhite | SponsoredSponsored Learn More Undo Born Before 1970? B routine priorityPublic-health news

Newborn one of 2 measles-associated deaths in Pennsylvania - CIDRAP

News Brief
In Pennsylvania, two measles-related deaths were reported, including a newborn, marking the first fatalities of the year in the United States. The deaths are associated with a measles outbreak that began earlier this year in Lancaster County, where both individuals were unvaccinated. The outbreak is feared to be undercounted, raising concerns about the broader spread of the virus. CIDRAP and public health authorities are monitoring the situation, emphasizing the need for vaccination to prevent further cases. The report highlights the risks posed by unvaccinated communities and the importance of addressing vaccine hesitancy to control the outbreak.

宾夕法尼亚州35年来首次记录麻疹死亡病例:兰开斯特县两人死亡 - Tech Times

Measles Kills Two in Lancaster County: Pennsylvania Records First Deaths in 35 Years - Tech Times
新闻简报2026年,宾夕法尼亚州在35年来首次报告与麻疹相关的死亡病例,该州兰开斯特县两名未接种疫苗的居民因该疾病死亡。这也是当年美国首次出现此类死亡病例。此次暴发疫情为30年来最严重,全州29个县共报告393例确诊病例,70例住院病例。兰开斯特县幼儿园儿童的疫苗接种接种率从2017年的95.5%降至2025–26年的87.6%,低于群体免疫阈值。康复者的免疫系统可能在长达3年内受损,增加其他感染的风险。此次死亡事件发生在联邦政策调整期间,该政策缩减了儿童疫苗的推荐接种时间表,并将MMR 疫苗分开接种,公共卫生专家认为此举危险。该州法律允许学校疫苗接种的自动豁免,导致暴发疫情。宾夕法尼亚州卫生部门确认了死亡病例,而CDC的领导层和指导工作受政策变化影响,包括解散免疫实践咨询委员会及解雇其主任。
查看新闻五要素
时间
宾夕法尼亚州的麻疹 暴发 于2026年报告,其中两例死亡发生在周二的兰开斯特县,如N2所述。在八年间观察到疫苗接种率下降,2025–26学年显著降至87.6%(N9)。2026年全国麻疹疫苗接种率为92.4%(N19)。
地点与对象
该事件发生于宾夕法尼亚州兰开斯特县,影响未接种疫苗的居民。该县2025–26学年幼儿园麻疹 疫苗接种率降至87.6%,超过八分之一的儿童未获得保护(N9)。暴发在宾夕法尼亚州393个确诊病例和70例住院病例中跨越29个县传播(N6)。
事件
宾夕法尼亚州兰开斯特县两名未接种疫苗的居民因麻疹死亡,这是2026年美国首次出现麻疹相关死亡病例,也是该州35年来首次记录此类死亡病例(N2)。宾夕法尼亚州卫生部门确认了这两起死亡病例(N3)。
规模、影响与风险
暴发疫情在宾夕法尼亚州导致393例确诊病例,其中20%住院,且所有住院患者均未完成全程疫苗接种(N20)。康复者的免疫系统可能在两到三年内受到抑制,增加感染其他疾病的风险(N24)。麻疹病毒的基本再生数(R0)为12至18,表明其传染性极高(N9)。
应对、状态与不确定性
公共卫生官员和医生批评联邦政策变化削弱了疫苗接种基础设施(N27)。CDC的作用受到领导层变动和职位空缺的影响(N32)。疫苗接种接种率下降被归因于州法律允许自动豁免(N12)。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Measles Kills Two in Lancaster County: Pennsylvania Records First Deaths in 35 Years - Tech Times

News Brief
In 2026, Pennsylvania recorded its first measles-associated deaths in 35 years, with two unvaccinated residents in Lancaster County succumbing to the disease. This marks the first such deaths in the U.S. that year. The outbreak, the worst in three decades, saw 393 confirmed cases across 29 counties and 70 hospitalizations statewide. The vaccination rate for kindergartners in Lancaster County dropped from 95.5% in 2017 to 87.6% in 2025–26, below the herd immunity threshold. Survivors face a weakened immune system for up to three years, increasing their risk of other infections. The deaths occurred amid a federal policy shift that reduced the recommended childhood vaccine schedule and split the MMR vaccine, which public health experts deemed dangerous. The state's law allows automatic exemptions for school vaccinations, contributing to the outbreak. The Pennsylvania Department of Health confirmed the deaths, while the CDC's leadership and guidance were impacted by policy changes, including the removal of the Advisory Committee on Immunization Practices and the firing of its director.
Original source text (English)
Dr. Jeffrey Gunzenhauser, Interim Health Officer from the Los Angeles County Department of Public Health, briefs the media outside the Department of Public Health in Los Angeles, California on February 4, 2015, with a general update of the measles outbreak in Los Angeles County. FREDERIC J. BROWN/AFP via Getty Images Measles claimed two lives in Lancaster County, Pennsylvania, on Tuesday — the first measles-associated deaths in the United States in 2026 and the first in the Commonwealth in 35 years. The Pennsylvania Department of Health confirmed both victims were unvaccinated residents of a county where the kindergarten measles vaccination rate has fallen nearly eight percentage points in eight years, sinking well below the level required to prevent community spread of one of the most contagious airborne diseases known to medicine. "My deepest sympathies are with the loved ones who are facing this unimaginable loss," Pennsylvania Secretary of Health Dr. Debra Bogen said in a written statement. "Because measles was largely eliminated in the Commonwealth for more than three decades, people are not familiar with this disease and don't fully understand the potential severity of the illness." The deaths arrived in the middle of Pennsylvania's worst measles outbreak in three decades , with 393 confirmed cases spread across 29 counties and 70 hospitalizations statewide. They also arrived 15 days after President Trump signed an executive order pushing to narrow the recommended childhood vaccine schedule from 17 shots to 11 and to split the combination measles-mumps-rubella vaccine into three separate injections — a change that public health experts, including the American Academy of Pediatrics, called "not only disheartening but dangerous." How Did Lancaster County's Vaccination Rate Fall This Far? Lancaster County's kindergarten MMR vaccination rate stood at 95.5% in 2017 — just above the 95% threshold public health officials call the minimum required to prevent measles from spreading through a community. By the 2025–26 school year, it had fallen to 87.6% , leaving more than one in eight children entering school entirely unprotected from a disease with a basic reproduction number (R0) of 12 to 18 — meaning each infected person can pass it to up to 18 unprotected bystanders in a shared space. That 7.9-percentage-point decline did not happen randomly. Pennsylvania is among 15 states that allow parents to exempt their children from required school vaccinations by citing religious grounds or "a strong moral or ethical conviction similar to a religious belief" under 28 Pa. Code §23.84 . The law requires only a written statement — no physician signature, no documentation of the specific belief, no review by any health authority. School nurses across the state have confirmed that these exemptions are effectively automatic. Joshua Good, who heads Ephrata Mennonite School in Lancaster County — where fewer than half of kindergartners were immunized against measles last year — described a decade-long shift in parental attitudes: "The opposition to vaccination has become entrenched in a significant portion of parents, with concerns ranging from how vaccines are developed to their safety to resentment over the handling of immunizations and public restrictions during the COVID-19 pandemic." Good specifically named Health and Human Services Secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr. as a contributing factor: "And then you throw in messaging from key health people like RFK Jr." Kennedy spoke to roughly 1,500 people in Lancaster County in 2021, at a time when he was a prominent private-sector anti-vaccine activist, and voiced skepticism about vaccines . Nationally, the picture is equally bleak. During the 2025–26 school year, 4.2% of US kindergartners — approximately 155,000 children — had at least one vaccine exemption, up from 3.6% the prior year, according to CDC data published in August 2026 . Vaccine exemptions rose in 41 states and the District of Columbia. The national MMR kindergarten vaccination rate stands at 92.4%, down from 92.5% the prior year — still below the 95% herd immunity threshold. What the Outbreak Has Done to Patients — and What Comes After Of the 393 Pennsylvanians confirmed with measles so far in 2026, roughly 20% have been hospitalized, and none had been fully vaccinated . Children made up approximately one-third of the state's cases. Dr. Fahmida McGann, an infectious disease specialist at Penn State Health Lancaster Medical Center, told a state press conference in June that hospitalized patients this summer have experienced electrolyte abnormalities, kidney and liver dysfunction, and blood count abnormalities, leaving them "exposed to serious organ failures, secondary infections, as well as bleeding." For survivors, the risk does not end with recovery. Research by Dr. Michael Mina and colleagues, published in the journal Science in 2019, documented a phenomenon researchers call "immune amnesia": measles virus infects and destroys preexisting memory lymphocytes — the cells that carry the immune system's learned response to other diseases — reducing unvaccinated children's antibody repertoire by 11% to 73% . The suppression lasts approximately two to three years, during which survivors are systematically more vulnerable to every infection they had previously been vaccinated against or survived — from chickenpox to bacterial pneumonia to strep throat. The MMR vaccine does not produce this effect; only infection with the actual measles virus does. The practical consequence: the survivors among the 393 Pennsylvanians who contracted measles this year have not simply "recovered." Each is now carrying a depleted immune system for the next two to three years, more vulnerable to all other infectious diseases that immunity had previously defended them against, as researchers at ASM document in detail . How the Federal Policy Landscape Made This Worse The Lancaster County deaths occurred in a federal policy environment that public health officials and physicians say has systematically undermined the vaccination infrastructure that eliminated measles from the United States in 2000. Kennedy has held a formal position encouraging parents to vaccinate against measles since the 2025 Texas outbreak claimed two children's lives, but his earlier advocacy shaped attitudes in communities like Lancaster County long before those statements. In November 2025, he ordered the CDC to rewrite language on its website suggesting vaccines may cause autism — a change Kennedy personally directed that directly contradicts decades of peer-reviewed epidemiology. He disbanded the Advisory Committee on Immunization Practices, the expert panel that translates FDA vaccine approvals into clinical guidance. He fired CDC Director Susan Monarez in August 2025 after she refused to implement his vaccine directives, and four senior agency officials resigned alongside her. As of early 2026, 80% of top CDC positions were vacant — eliminating the leadership needed to coordinate emergency responses. On August 10, President Trump intensified that pressure by signing an executive order calling for the number of universally recommended childhood vaccines to be cut from 17 to 11, and specifically directing the administration to split the half-century-old MMR combination vaccine into three separate injections. There is no scientific evidence that splitting the MMR provides benefit , according to Johns Hopkins Bloomberg School of Public Health. The order also directed the Department of Justice to file lawsuits against states that fail to provide adequate religious or medical vaccine exemptions, and directed the Department of Education to cut grants to those states. The executive order was the Trump administration's second attempt to alter the childhood vaccine schedule — a prior schedule attempt had been blocked by a federal court. Pennsylvania Gov. Josh Shapiro (D) rejected any change to the state's vaccination guidelines. "President Donald Trump and [Health and Human Services Secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr.] are screwing around with the childhood vaccine schedule — ignoring decades of science, sowing chaos and confusion, and spreading dangerous misinformation that puts kids and families at risk," Shapiro said. Dr. Andrew Racine, president of the American Academy of Pediatrics, called the executive order "not only disheartening but dangerous" at a moment when US measles cases had already reached a 35-year high. By that point, 28 states announced they would no longer follow federal vaccine recommendations. What Pennsylvania's 393 Cases Mean Nationally As of August 20, the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention had confirmed 2,777 measles cases in the United States in 2026 — a 35-year high that already surpasses all of 2025's 2,289 cases, themselves the highest annual total in more than three decades. Pennsylvania ranked third among all states in 2026 case counts, behind Utah and South Carolina. Three people died of measles in 2025 — two children in Texas and an unvaccinated adult in New Mexico — all of whom were unvaccinated and none of whom had known underlying conditions. Pennsylvania's deaths are the first measles fatalities confirmed in the United States in 2026. Their arrival changes the political and public health stakes of the outbreak in two ways: it demonstrates that 2026 is not a "high case count, zero deaths" year in which the national consequences of declining vaccination could be argued to be limited; and it establishes Pennsylvania — a large, politically contested state — as the site of measles mortality that can no longer be attributed solely to geographic isolation or extreme under-vaccination in specific sectarian communities. The Pan American Health Organization is scheduled to conduct a formal review of US measles elimination status at its November 2026 annual meeting, with experts widely expecting it to revoke the status the United States has held since 2000. What Does Pennsylvania's Exemption Law Actually Say? Under 28 Pa. Code §23.84, children in Pennsylvania are not required to be vaccinated if "the parent, guardian or emancipated child objects in writing to the immunization on religious grounds or on the basis of a strong moral or ethical conviction similar to a religious belief." The statute requires only a written objection — no physician consultation, no documentation of the specific religious belief, no county health authority review, and no re-certification period. The exemption covers all required school vaccinations, including MMR. This framework creates two structural vulnerabilities. First, it is effectively equivalent to a personal belief exemption — any strongly worded letter citing moral conviction qualifies, making the barrier to exemption lower than in most states that permit non-medical exemptions at all. Second, unvaccinated children are not randomly distributed across the population: they concentrate in specific schools, communities, and geographic areas. Lancaster County's Ephrata Mennonite School — where fewer than half of kindergartners were vaccinated — is a documented example. When unvaccinated children cluster, measles R0 mathematics guarantee that a single case can ignite sustained community transmission. Herd immunity is not a statewide average; it is a local condition that fails the moment local coverage falls below the threshold, regardless of what the rest of the state does. Dr. Amesh Adalja, a senior scholar at the Johns Hopkins Center for Health Security, has called measles "the canary in the coal mine" for vaccination infrastructure — the first disease to resurge when community immunity slips, precisely because its extreme contagiousness amplifies even small gaps in coverage. How to Check Your Measles Vaccination Status — and What to Do Pennsylvania health officials, responding to Tuesday's deaths, renewed urgent guidance for residents across the state: Check your vaccination status. Anyone who received only a single MMR dose, whose vaccination history is unknown, or who was vaccinated between 1963 and 1968 with the killed (inactivated) measles vaccine — which was less effective than the live attenuated vaccine that replaced it — should speak with a physician about whether a current MMR dose is needed. Adults born before 1957 are generally presumed immune through natural exposure. Adults born from 1957 to 1989 likely received only one dose; if facing ongoing exposure, confirming two-dose status is recommended. Infants in affected counties can receive early MMR vaccination. The state health department has recommended that infants in outbreak-affected counties receive an early MMR dose at six months — earlier than the standard 12-month schedule — as added protection during the ongoing outbreak. Call ahead before seeking care. Anyone who suspects measles exposure or recognizes symptoms — fever, cough, runny nose, red watery eyes — should call their healthcare provider or emergency room before arriving in person, to avoid exposing unprotected patients in waiting areas. The Pennsylvania Department of Health's confidential health hotline is available toll-free at 877-PA-HEALTH. If unvaccinated and recently exposed, act quickly. The MMR vaccine can be administered within 72 hours of exposure to reduce the risk of developing measles. Immune globulin, which can modify disease severity, can be given within six days. The statewide school vaccination lookup tool , which allows residents to check the MMR coverage at specific schools, remains available through the Pennsylvania Department of Health. Frequently Asked Questions Why were these the first measles deaths in Pennsylvania in 35 years if the disease has been circulating? Prior to 2026, Pennsylvania had not seen a sustained measles outbreak of this magnitude in decades, and the country's overall high vaccination rates kept even occasional cases from reaching the scale needed to produce fatalities. The three US measles deaths in 2025 — two children in Texas and an adult in New Mexico — occurred in communities with unusually low vaccination rates, some as low as 82% for kindergartners in Gaines County, Texas. Lancaster County's 87.6% kindergarten rate sits in that same danger zone — high enough that individual parents may feel safe opting out, but low enough that the mathematics of measles transmission (an R0 of up to 18 in susceptible populations) guarantee that a single introduced case will find enough unprotected hosts to sustain community spread. That is exactly what has been happening since late April, and Tuesday's deaths are the endpoint of that chain. What happens to someone who contracts measles and recovers — is the risk really over after the rash fades? No, and this is one of the most underreported consequences of the current outbreak. Research published in Science in 2019 by Dr. Michael Mina and colleagues documented that measles infection causes "immune amnesia" — the measles virus specifically targets and destroys memory lymphocytes, the cells that carry the immune system's learned defenses against other diseases. Unvaccinated children who contracted measles lost 11% to 73% of their preexisting antibody repertoire — including immunity to diseases they had been vaccinated against, such as chickenpox and whooping cough. The suppression lasts two to three years. This means every survivor of the 393 Pennsylvania cases confirmed this year is now more vulnerable to a broad range of other infections than they were before contracting measles, for a period that extends years into the future. The MMR vaccine does not cause this effect. Does Pennsylvania's exemption law need to change to prevent future outbreaks, and what would that require? Pennsylvania's 28 Pa. Code §23.84 allows parents to exempt children from all required school vaccinations with a written statement citing religious grounds or "a strong moral or ethical conviction similar to a religious belief" — no documentation, review, or physician signature required. Eighteen states that previously permitted personal belief or philosophical exemptions have tightened or eliminated them in recent years, often in response to outbreaks. New York removed its religious exemption in 2019 following measles outbreaks that affected thousands. Changing Pennsylvania's law would require action by the General Assembly; Gov. Shapiro has not publicly proposed legislation to eliminate the exemption, though he has rejected the federal vaccine order. What can change without legislation: school districts and health departments can actively audit exemption applications and notify families of local outbreak risks, rather than processing exemptions automatically. Is the measles vaccine safe, and does immunity from two doses last a lifetime? Yes and yes, for the overwhelming majority of recipients. The two-dose MMR schedule, typically administered at 12–15 months and 4–6 years, provides approximately 97% protection against measles — and the evidence strongly supports that this protection is long-lasting. Dr. Paul Offit, director of the Vaccine Education Center at Children's Hospital of Philadelphia, directly rebutted the claim, associated with HHS Secretary Kennedy, that MMR immunity wanes significantly, noting that sustained measles elimination in the US from 2000 until 2025 — 25 years — would have been impossible if immunity had waned as claimed. A 2026 systematic review published in The Lancet found mRNA vaccines safe across billions of administered doses; the MMR, in use for more than 50 years, has an even longer and more extensive safety record

Maryland declares measles outbreak in 3 counties - NBC4 Washington
新闻简报2026年8月24日,马里兰州卫生部在圣玛丽斯县、卡罗尔县和塞西尔县宣布麻疹疫情。在未接种疫苗的居民中确认了14例病例。这使得马里兰州今年麻疹病例总数达到31例,所有病例均发生在未接种疫苗的个体中。大多数病例与家庭内接触相关,未发现社区广泛传播的证据。卫生官员强调疫苗接种的重要性,指出MMR疫苗广泛可及且大多数保险覆盖。无保险人员可通过马里兰州儿童疫苗计划和马里兰州疫苗计划获得疫苗。与麻疹患者接触者应在21天内监测症状,如出现症状立即隔离,并在就医前联系医疗服务提供者。

B routine priorityPublic-health news

Maryland declares measles outbreak in 3 counties - NBC4 Washington

News Brief
On August 24, 2026, the Maryland Department of Health declared a measles outbreak in St. Mary’s, Carroll, and Cecil counties, identifying 14 confirmed cases among unvaccinated residents. This brings Maryland's total measles cases for the year to 31, all occurring in unvaccinated individuals. Most cases were linked to household contacts, with no evidence of widespread community transmission. Health officials emphasized the importance of vaccination, noting that the MMR vaccine is widely available and covered by most insurance. Uninsured individuals can access the vaccine through Maryland’s Vaccines for Children Program and the Maryland Vaccine Program. Those exposed to measles should monitor for symptoms for 21 days and isolate immediately if symptoms appear, while also contacting healthcare providers before visiting in person.

By Jordan Young • Published August 24, 2026 • Updated on August 24, 2026 at 6:22 pm Log in or create a free profile to save articles BOOKMARKER Play 0:00 Play 0:00 / 1:28 Unmute Subtitles EnglishNoneSettings ShareToggle Fullscreen NBC Universal, Inc. The Maryland Department of Health declared a measles outbreak in St. Mary’s, Carroll and Cecil counties. The Maryland Department of Health has declared a measles outbreak in St. Mary’s, Carroll and Cecil counties, health officials said Monday. The health department said it is investigating 14 confirmed cases among unvaccinated Maryland residents and that most of the case contacts occurred within specific households. Stream NBC4 newscasts for free right here, right now. WATCH HERE "I do want to reinforce right now that there is currently no evidence of widespread community transmission in Maryland or in those counties, and most cases that we've seen have occurred among household contacts," Dr. Meg Sullivan, deputy secretary for MDH, told News4. Measles is considered highly contagious and can be spread through the air when an infected person breathes, sneezes or coughs. It can also cause serious health complications or even death, according to the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention. So far this year, Maryland has recorded 31 measles cases as of Aug. 24, all among people who are not vaccinated, health officials said. Twenty-seven of those cases were identified since July 15. Health 4 on Tour 4 hours ago Community groups highlight women's health, physical activity in Prince George's Crime and Courts 17 hours ago Lindsay Clancy trial: Psychiatrist weighs in on Clancy's mental health In 2025, Maryland only recorded three measles cases. The uptick comes as the U.S. marks the highest number of cases nationwide in 35 years. Preventing measles Maryland health officials are encouraging residents to get vaccinated for measles, emphasizing if an unvaccinated person is exposed to measles, there is a 90% chance they will be infected. To check if you are protected, you can request your vaccination record through MDH here or with your health provider. You can also find information about vaccine clinics in your area here. Most standard insurance will cover the MMR vaccine, which is used to prevent measles, according to the health department. But those who are uninsured can also access the vaccine through Maryland’s Vaccines for Children Program and the Maryland Vaccine Program. "For those individuals that are up to date with their MMR vaccine, the risk remains low," Sullivan said. "So I do think for parents and for families, what we want them to be aware of the information that we shared today, but also to stress that the most effective way to protect yourself is to be up to date with the MMR vaccine and that it is widely available." People who are at risk and have been exposed should watch out for symptoms for 21 days after the exposure. If symptoms appear, isolate immediately and then call your healthcare provider. If it is necessary to see the healthcare provider in person or go to the emergency room, call ahead of time so they can protect staff and other patients. 1:14 What is measles? Measles is a highly contagious airborne disease caused by a virus that can lead to severe complications and death. What are the symptoms? Symptoms of measles develop over two stages. The first stage typically begins seven to 14 days after exposure. Here are some common symptoms: Fever over 101 degrees Runny nose Red, watery eyes Cough The second stage starts three to five days after the initial symptoms begin and is marked by a rash on the face, which then spreads to the rest of the body. A person with measles is contagious from four days before this rash appears until four days after it appeared. 密苏里州卫生部敦促加强麻疹疫苗接种 - Spectrum News

Missouri Health pushing measles vaccinations - Spectrum News
新闻简报The Missouri Department of Health and Senior Services (MO-DHSS) is urging increased measles vaccinations due to the state's susceptibility to outbreaks. As of 2026-08-27, Missouri has reported five measles cases this year, compared to six last year. The department emphasized that vaccination rates are below the 95% threshold needed for herd immunity, which increases the risk of rapid spread. One measles case can infect nine out of 10 unvaccinated individuals, and the virus can linger in the air for up to two hours after exposure. Officials highlighted that two doses of the MMR vaccine provide 97% lifetime protection and that it’s never too late to vaccinate. The MO-DHSS is promoting vaccination efforts to mitigate the risk of further outbreaks, but the full extent of the situation and response measures remain under investigation. The virus can cause severe complications such as pneumonia, brain swelling, and even death, underscoring the urgency of vaccination campaigns.
查看新闻五要素
时间
密苏里州卫生与老龄事务部(MO-DHSS)于2026-08-27发布声明称,该州今年已报告麻疹例病例,去年为6例。该病毒在暴露后最长可在空气中存活两小时。
地点与对象
密苏里州,重点关注圣路易斯及麻疹疫苗接种率低于实现群体免疫所需95%阈值的社区。
事件
密苏里州卫生与老龄服务部(MO-DHSS)敦促加强麻疹疫苗接种,以应对该州对麻疹暴发的易感性。该部门指出,一例麻疹病例可感染10名未接种疫苗者中的9人,且病毒在咳嗽或打喷嚏后可在空气中滞留长达2小时。
规模、影响与风险
密苏里州今年已报告5例麻疹病例,去年同期为6例。由于麻疹疫苗接种率低于实现群体免疫所需的95%阈值,麻疹暴发风险升高。该病毒可引发严重并发症,如肺炎、脑肿胀,甚至死亡。
应对、状态与不确定性
密苏里州卫生与老龄服务部(MO-DHSS)正在推进麻疹疫苗接种工作,并强调两剂麻疹-腮腺炎-风疹(MMR)疫苗可提供97%的终身保护。官员们表示,接种疫苗永远不会太晚,但当前麻疹暴发风险的全面影响及应对措施仍在调查中。

Missouri Health pushing measles vaccinations - Spectrum News

News Brief
The Missouri Department of Health and Senior Services (MO-DHSS) is urging increased measles vaccinations due to the state's susceptibility to outbreaks. As of 2026-08-27, Missouri has reported five measles cases this year, compared to six last year. The department emphasized that vaccination rates are below the 95% threshold needed for herd immunity, which increases the risk of rapid spread. One measles case can infect nine out of 10 unvaccinated individuals, and the virus can linger in the air for up to two hours after exposure. Officials highlighted that two doses of the MMR vaccine provide 97% lifetime protection and that it’s never too late to vaccinate. The MO-DHSS is promoting vaccination efforts to mitigate the risk of further outbreaks, but the full extent of the situation and response measures remain under investigation. The virus can cause severe complications such as pneumonia, brain swelling, and even death, underscoring the urgency of vaccination campaigns.
Original source text (English)
MISSOURI—The Missouri Department of Health and Senior Services (MO-DHSS) says more of Missouri needs to receive vaccinations for measles, saying that the state is susceptible to an outbreak risk. “We’re below the 95% needed for herd immunity, which means outbreaks can spread fast in our communities,” said the department. “One measles case can infect nine out of 10 unvaccinated people, and the virus can hang in the air for up to two hours after someone coughs or sneezes.” Missouri already has five measles cases this year, according to the CDC . Last year, it had six. “Two doses of the MMR (measles, mumps and rubella) vaccine give 97% lifetime protection," officials said, adding that it’s never too late to vaccinate. Illinois has seen its measles cases drop over the last three years from 67 in 2024 to 14 last year and 4 so far this year. According to Illinois Department of Public Health , nearly 97% of its population was vaccinated for measles as of May this year. Though commonly seen as an irritating rash, the health department notes the virus may cause pneumonia, brain swelling and even death

Scale, impact and risk

Response, status and uncertainty
The Missouri Department of Health and Senior Services (MO-DHSS) is promoting measles vaccinations and emphasizing the importance of two doses of the MMR vaccine for 97% lifetime protection. Officials stated it’s never too late to vaccinate, but the full extent of the outbreak risk and response measures remain under investigation.

肯塔基州麻疹疫情升至29例,为1991年来最高 - LEX 18新闻

Kentucky measles outbreak grows to 29 cases, most since 1991 - LEX 18 News
新闻简报肯塔基州正在经历一场麻疹暴发,2026年已确诊29例,为1991年来最高。州卫生官员报告,自上周起新增10例,其中17例在8月单月确诊。美国疾病控制与预防中心(CDC)将此次疫情定义为3例或以上相关病例。所有报告病例均为未接种疫苗或疫苗接种状态不明的个体。目前尚无死亡病例报告。麻疹是一种高度传染性呼吸道疾病,通过感染者咳嗽或打喷嚏时的空气传播。症状包括发热、咳嗽、流涕、眼睛发红或流泪以及皮疹。卫生官员敦促肯塔基州居民核查自身疫苗接种状态,并就麻疹、腮腺炎及风疹(MMR)疫苗接种咨询医疗服务提供者。CDC建议儿童接种两剂MMR疫苗,可提供约97%的麻疹保护效果。肯塔基州公共卫生部门在确认新病例后定期更新麻疹监测仪表板。官员警告称,随着调查继续,可能还会发现更多病例。

29例确诊病例,自上周起新增10例,8月单月确诊17例。目前尚无死亡病例报告。CDC将此次疫情定义为3例或以上相关病例。麻疹是一种高度传染性呼吸道疾病,通过空气传播。

B routine priorityPublic-health news

Kentucky measles outbreak grows to 29 cases, most since 1991 - LEX 18 News

News Brief
Kentucky is experiencing a measles outbreak with 29 confirmed cases in 2026, the highest number since 1991. State health officials report that 10 new cases have been added since last week, with 17 cases identified in August alone. The outbreak is defined by the CDC as three or more related cases. All reported cases involve individuals who are unvaccinated or have unknown vaccination status. No deaths have been reported. Measles is a highly contagious respiratory illness that spreads through the air when an infected person coughs or sneezes. Symptoms include fever, cough, runny nose, red or watery eyes, and a rash. Health officials are urging Kentuckians to review their vaccination status and consult healthcare providers about the measles, mumps, and rubella (MMR) vaccine. The CDC recommends two doses of the vaccine for children, providing about 97% protection against measles. The Kentucky Department for Public Health updates its measles dashboard regularly as new cases are confirmed. Officials have warned that additional cases may be identified as investigations continue.
Original source text (English)
